> eftertanke. Alle byens klokker brøt ut i det samme og
> ringet til vesper, men gutterne snakket i munden paa
> hverandre og skulde peke ut alt hun saa —.
> Tautra kunde hun ikke se, for der gik en vindrosse
> over fjorden indunder Frosta med skodde og sopende
> regnskurer.
> Midt i ungeflokken gik hun nedover de steile stier
> gjennem Steinbergene — og nu ringet kubjelder og
> gjæterer kavet rundt omkring — kjørne drog hjem
> fra bymarken. Ved porten i virket over Nidareid maatte
> Kristin og hendes unge følgesvender bie, mens feet
> blev drevet igjennem — gjæterne hojet, ropte og
> skjældte, okser stangedes, kjør trængtes mellem hin
> anden, og gutterne nævnte hvem som eiet denne eller
> hin stuten. Og da de var gjennem porten og gik ind
> over mot gjeilerne, fik Kristin mere end nok med
> at se, hvor hun satte sine nakne føtter mellem ku
> rukkerne i det optraakkede raak.
> Nogen av gutterne fulgte hende übedt helt ind i
> Kristkirken. Og da hun stod i den skumre søileskog
> og saa frem mot lysene og guldet i koret, drog gut
> terne hele tiden i fremmedkonen og skulde vise hende
> — fra de farvede lysflekker som solen paa rosevinduet
> kastet ind mellem hvælvene, likstener paa gulvet, til
> himlingerne av dyre tøier over alterne — alle slike
> ting som mest falder børn i øinene. Kristin fik ingen
> fred til at samle sine tanker — men hvert ord som
> gutterne sa vakte den dumpe tråa i hendes hjerte —
> efter sønnerne først og fremst, men efter gaarden og
> saa, stuerne, uthusene, feet — morens stræv og morens
> herredømme.
> Hun hadde endda denne uvilje mot at bli gjenkjendt
> av nogen som hadde været hendes og Erlends venner
> før i verden. De pleiet altid være i bygaardene sine
> under festen og ha gjester hos sig — hun gruet for at
> støte paa et følge av dem. Ulf Haldorssøn maatte hun
> opsøke, for han hadde styrt som hendes ombudsmand
> med de gaardparterne som hun endda eiet her norden
> fjelds og nu vilde gi for sit provent i Reins kloster.
> Men nu hadde han vel frænderne sine fra gaarden i
> Skaun hos sig, saa hun fik vente. Men hun visste
> at en mand som hadde tjent mellem Erlends hærmænd
> under hans sysselmandstid skulde bo i en liten gaard
> ute paa Bratøren, han drev jagt paa hvitskjæving og
> nise i fjorden og holdt herberg for sjøbønder.
> Her var overfyldt i alle hus, blev der svart hende,
> men saa kom manden seiv, Aamunde, til og kjendte
> hende straks. Det var rart at høre ham rope hendes
> gamle navn:
> «Nu mener jeg — er det ikke Erlend Nikulaussøns
> husfrue av Husaby — hilsæl, Kristin — hvordan det
> kan bære til at du kommer hit i gaarden min!»
> Han var bare glad til, at hun vilde tækkes med
> slikt nattely som han kunde byde hende, og han lovet
> 30 —Kristin. 111.
> at han seiv skulde seile hende ut til Tautra dagen
> efter festen.
> Til langt paa nat sat hun ute i gaarden og snak
> ket med huskarlen, og det tok hende sterkt da hun
> merket, Erlends fordums mænd elsket og hædret høit
> mindet om sin unge høvding — Aamunde brukte flere
> ganger det ordet om ham, ung. Fra Ulf Haldorssøn
> visste de om hans ulykkelige død, og Aamunde sa, han
> møttes aldrig med nogen av sine gamle fæller fra
> Husaby-tiden, uten de dråk sin djerve husbonds minde
> — og to ganger hadde nogen av dem skutt sammen
> og latt læse messe efter ham paa hans dødsdag.
> Aamunde spurte meget efter Erlendssønnerne, og saa
> spurte Kristin efter gamle kjendte. Det blev midnat før
> hun kom iseng ved siden av Aamundes hustru — han
> vilde endelig at de begge skulde rømmet sengen sin
> for hende, og sidst maatte hun med tak ta imot hans
> plads ialfald.
> Næste dag var Olavsvaka. Fra morgenstunden gik
> Kristin nede paa ørerne, saa paa travlheten ved bryg
> gerne. Hendes hjerte tok til at banke, da hun saa herr
> abbeden av Tautra stige iland — men de munkene som
> fulgte ham var alle ældre mænd.
> Alt før nonstid strømmet folket mot Kristkirken, bæ
> rende og støttende sine syke og krøplinger for at faa
> slik plads til dem i langhuset, at de kunde komme nær
> skrinet, naar det blev baaret ut i procession næste dag
> efter høimessen.
> Da Kristin kom op mellem boderne som var slaat
> op ved kirkegaardsgjærdet — der blev mest solgt mat
> og drikke, vokslys og hynder flettet av siv eller bjerke
> kvister til at lægge under sig paa kirkegulvet — raa
> ket hun paa Andebyggerne, og Kristin tok barnet, mens
> den unge konen fik sig en drik mungaat. I det samme
> kom toget av de engelske pilegrimmer med sang og
> merker og tændte lys; i den forvirring som blev da
> disse skulde gjennem de sammenstappede menneske
> masser ved boderne, mistet hun Andebyggerne, og siden
> kunde hun ikke finde dem igjen.
> Længe vildret hun hit og dit i utkanten av folke
> mugen og bysset det skrikende barnet. Da hun la dets
> ansigt ind til halsen sin og kjælte for at trøste det,
> søkte det og suget sin mund mot hendes hud; hun
> skjønte at det var tørst og visste ikke sin arme raad.
> Moren syntes det faafængt at lete efter, hun maatte
> gaa ned i gaterne og høre om hun kunde faa melk til
> det. Men da hun kom ut i Øvre Langstræte og vilde
> gaa nordover, blev der igjen stor trængsel — sydfra
> kom et rytterfølge og samtidig toget borgsvendene fra
> kongsgaarden ind paa pladsen mellem kirken og kors
> brødregaarden. Kristin blev trængt ind i den nærme
> ste veiten, men ogsaa her for ridende og, gaaende mot
> kirken, og stimen blev saa svær, saa sidst maatte hun
> redde sig op paa en stengar.
> Luften over hende fyldtes med klokkeklang — fra
> domkirken ringet nona hora. Barnet holdt op at skrike
> ved lyden — saa op mot himmelen, og der kom et
> skjær av forstand i dets sløve øine — det smilte litt.
> Bevæget bøiet den gamle mor sig ned og kysset det
> arme krypet. Da saa hun at hun sat paa stengjærdet
> omkring humlehagen til Nikulausgaarden, den gamle
> bygaarden deres.
> — Hun skulde vel kjende den murede skorstenen op
> gjennem torvtaket — baksiden av stuehuset deres. Nær
> mest hende laa sykehusets gaarder som Erlend harmet
> sig slik over hadde faat ret til hagen sammen med
> dem.
> Hun knuget den fremmede kvindes barn til sit bryst,
> kysset og kysset det. Da tok nogen hende paa knæet —.
> En munk i prædikebrødrenes hvite kutte og svarte
> hættekappe. Hun saa ned i et gulblekt, rynket gam
> melmandsansigt, — en sammenfalden smal og lang
> mund, to store ravgule øine som laa dypt indsunket.
> «Kan dette være — er dette dig seiv, Kristin Lav
> ransdatter!» Munken la de korslagte armer paa sten
> gjærdet og boret sit hode nedi. «— Er du her!»
> «Gunnulf!» Da flyttet han hodet, saa det rørte hen
> des knæ, der hun sat: «Tykkes dig det være saa un
> derlig at jeg er her —» Da kom det hende i hugen,
> at hun sat paa gjærdet til denne gaarden som hadde
> været hans først og siden hendes eget hus, og hun
> syntes, det var rart likevel.
> «Men hvad er det for et barn du har i knæet
> det er nu vel ikke sønnen til Gaute han der?»
> «Nei —» ved tanken paa lille Erlends sunde, søte
> aasyn og sterke, velskapte krop krystet hun det frem
> mede vesalbarnet ind til sig, overvældet av medynk:
> «Det er barnet til en kone som jeg hadde følge med
> over fjeldet.»
> Men da dæmret det for hende, hvad Andres Si
> monssøn hadde set i sin barnslige visdom. Fuld av
> ærefrygt saa hun paa den ynkelige skapning som laa
> i hendes fang.
> Men nu graat det igjen, og hun maatte allerførs*
> spørre munken om han kunde si hende, hvor hun skulde
> faa noget melk til barnet. Gunnulf førte hende øst om
> kring kirken til Predikarliv og skaffet hende melk i ca
> bolle. Mens Kristin måtet sit fosterbarn, talte de sam
> men, men det gik underlig traat.
> «Saa lang tid er gaat og saa meget hændt siden
> sidst,» sa hun sørgmodig. «Og tunge var de vel at
> bære for dig og, de tidender som du spurte om bror
> din?»
> «Gud naade hans arme sjæl,» hvisket broder Gun
> nulf rystet.
> Først da hun spurte om sine sønner i Tautra, snak
> ket Gunnulf noget mere. Med hjertelig glæde hadde
> konventet tat imot de to novicer som var ættet fra lan
> dets bedste slegter. Nikulaus syntes at ha saa herlige
> aandsgaver og gjorde slike fremsteg i lærdom og guds
> frygt, at abbeden maatte mindes hans herlige ætfar,
> kirkens evnerike stridsmand biskop Nikulaus Arnessøn.
> Det var den første tiden. Men en stund efter at brø
> drene var blit indklædt hadde Nikulaus artet sig meget
> ilde og voldt stor uro i klosteret. Gunnulf visste ikke
> nøie grunden — en var at abbed Johannes vilde ikke
> tilstede, de unge brødrene fik prestevigelsen, før de
> hadde fyldt det tredivte aaret, og han vilde ikke vige
> fra denne regel med Nikulaus. Og da den værdige fa
> der tykte at Nikulaus læste og grublet mere end han
> hadde aandelig modenhet til og brøt ned sin helse med
> fromhetsøvelser, vilde han sende ham ind til en av klo
> sterets bugaarder paa Inderøen forat han der i lydig
> het under nogen av de ældre munkene skulde plante
> en eplehage. Da skulde Nikulaus ha brutt ut i ulydig
> het mot abbeden, ha beskyldt sine brødre for at de ved
> vellevnet hadde forfaret klosterets gods, for lathet til
> gudstjeneste og usømmelig tale. Det meste av saken
> kom jo ikke utenfor konventet, sa Gunnulf, men han
> skulde ogsaa ha sat sig til motverge mot den broderen
> som abbeden hadde sat til at refse ham. En tid lang
> hadde han ligget i carceret, visste Gunnulf, men sidst
> hadde han da faldt til fote, da abbeden truet med at
> skille ham fra broder Bjørgulf og sende den ene av
> dem til Munkabu — det var nok den blinde broren som
> satte ham op. Men da blev Nikulaus bodfærdig og spak.
> «Det er farens art som er i dem,» sa Gunnulf bit
> tert. «Andet var ikke ventelig end at mine brorsønner
> skulde ha tungt for at lære lydighet og vise ustadighet
> i et gudelig forsæt —»
> «Det kan like snart være morsarven deres,» svarte
> Kristin sorgfuldt. «Ulydighet var min hovedsynd, Gun
> nulf — og ustadig var jeg ogsaa. Alle mine levedager
> længtet jeg baade efter at gaa den rette veien og gaa
> mine egne vildstier like fuldt —»
> «Erlends vildstier, mener du,» sa munken mørkt.
> «Ikke en gang lokket min bror dig, Kristin, men jeg
> tror han lokket dig hver dag du levet med ham. Glemsk
> gjorde han dig saa du ikke sanset, naar du tænkte tan
> ker som du maatte blyges over, at for Gud den alvi
> dende kunde du ikke dølge hvad du tænkte —.»
> Kristin stirret ut for sig.
> «Nu vet jeg ikke, Gunnulf, om du har ret — ikke
> vet jeg om jeg nogen tid har glemt at Gud saa ind i
> mit hjerte, og større er vel min synd. Likevel er det
> ikke slik som du kanske tror at jeg mest trængte blyges
> for min üblyge kjæte og for min svakhet, men mere
> for at mine tanker om min husbond var mange ganger
> beskere end ormeedder. Men det sidste maatte vel
> komme efter det første — du var den som sa mig det
> engang, at de som har elsket hinanden med den heteste
> attraa, de ender med at være som to onner som biter
> hinanden i sporden.
> Men det har været min trøst i disse aarene, Gun
> nulf, saa ofte jeg tænkte paa at Erlend maatte gaa ind
> for Guds dom, uhuslet og uhjulpen, støtt ned med vrede
> i hjertet og blod paa hænderne — han blev ikke slik
> som du sa og som jeg blev. Han gjemte ikke mere
> paa vrede og uret end han gjemte andre ting. — Gun
> nulf, han var saa fager og han saa fredsæl ut da jeg
> hadde klædt hans lik — jeg vet at Gud den alvidende
> vet, Erlend har aldrig baaret nag til nogen mand og
> ikke for nogen sak.»
> Broren saa paa hende med vidt opspilte øine. Saa
> nikket han.
> Om en stund spurte munken:
> «Vet du at Eiliv Serkssøn er prest og nonnernes
> raadsmand ute paa Rein?»
> «Nei?» sa Kristin jublende lykkelig.
> «Jeg trodde det var derfor du hadde valgt at gi dig
> ind der,» sa Gunnulf. Like efter talte han om at han
> maatte gaa tilbake til sit kloster.
> Den første nocturne var begyndt, da Kristin kom ind
> i kirken. I langhuset og omkring alle altere va/r der
> en trængsel av folk, men en kirketjener, som saa at
> hun hadde et meget usselt barn i armene, tok og puffet
> hende frem i mugen, saa hun kom helt frem mellem
> krøplingerne og de sykestes skarer som var leiret midt
> i kirken under hovedtaarnets hvælv, med god utsig* til
> koret.
> Mange hundrede lys brændte i kirken — kirketjenerne
> tok imot pilegrimmenes kjerter og satte dem paa de
> saatedannede smaataarn overslaat med lysespiker, som
> stod stillet op nedigjennem kirken. Eftersom dagslyset
> døde ut bak de brogede glasruter, lunedes kirken ved
> lugten av det brændende voks, men smaat om senn
> fyldtes den ogsaa av den sure stank fra de sykes og
> fattiges filler.
> Naar korsangen tonet under hvælvene, og organdur,
> fløiter og bumber og strengeleker laat, skjønte Kristin
> hvorfor kirken monne være kaldet skib — i det mæg
> tige stenhus syntes alle disse mennesker være ombord
> i et fartøi, og sangen var som brus fra et hav, som
> det bares paa. Indimellem la det sig tilro som paa
> stilnende dønninger, naar en enkelt mandsrøst alene
> bar læsestykket ut over masserne.
> Ansigt ved ansigt, som blev mere hvite og trætte
> eftersom vaakenatten led. Næsten ingen gik ut mellem
> tjenesterne, ialfald ikke av de som hadde faat pladser
> midt nedigjennem kirken. I stunderne mellem noctur
> nerne døset de eller bad. Barnet sov næsten hele nat
> ten — etpar ganger maatte Kristin bysse det litt eller
> gi det melk av en træflaske, som Gunnulf hadde skaffet
> hende fra klosteret.
> Møtet med Erlends bror hadde revet hende under
> lig op — efter at hvert steg paa veien hit nord hadde
> ført hende nærmere og nærmere hjem til mindet om
> den døde. Hun hadde tænkt litet paa ham de sidste
> aarene, mens starvet for de fremvoksende sønnerne
> undte hende liten tid til at huske sin egen skjæbne —
> likevel hadde tanken paa ham altid været likesom like
> bak hende, bare at hun ikke hadde stunder til at snu
> sig dit. Nu tyktes hun se sin sjæl i de aarene; den
> hadde levet som folk lever paa gaardene det travle
> sommerhalvaaret, naar en flytter ut av storstuen og
> bor paa bursloftet. Men en gaar og løper forbi vinter
> stuen hele dagen, tænker aldrig paa at gaa dit ind,
> endda det er bare at ta i klinkefaldet og skyve op en
> dør. Og naar en endelig en dag faar et ærinde ditind,
> saa er stuen blit fremmed og næsten høitidsfuld av det
> at den har faat lugten av ensomhet og stille —.
> Men mens hun talte med den mand som var den sidste
> levende medvider til vekselspillet mellem utsæd og høst
> i hendes liv med den døde — da tyktes det hende at
> hun var kommet til at se utover sit liv paa en ny maate:
> som naar en kommer op paa en høide over hjembygden,
> hvor en aldrig har været før, og derfrå ser ned i sin
> egen dal. Hver gaard og hvert gjærde, hvert kjerr og
> hvert bækkefar kjender en, men en synes se for første
> gang, hvordan alting ligger paa det lænde som bærer
> bygden. Og ut av dette nye syn hadde hun medett
> fundet ord som løste op baade hendes bitterhet mot
> Erlend og hendes angst for hans sjæl som f6r bort i
> braadøden. Nag hadde han aldrig kjendt; hun saa det
> nu, og Gud hadde set det altid.
> Saa var hun da endelig naadd saa langt at hun
> tykte sig se sit eget liv likesom fra de øverste bræk
> kerne i et skar. Nu bar hendes vei nedover i skome
> dalen, men forinden hadde hun faat naade til at skjønne,
> i klosterets ensomhet og i dødens dør biet en paa
> hende som altid hadde set menneskenes liv som men
> neskenes bygder syner fra fjeldkanten. Han hadde set
> syndén og sorgen og kjærligheten og hatet i hjerterne
> som en ser de rike gaarder og fattige kot, bugnende
> akrer og gjenlagt ødeland baaret oppe paa det samme
> lænde. Og han hadde steget ned, hans føtter hadde
> gjennemvandret folklandene, staat i borgene og i hyt
> terne, samlet de rikes og de ringes sorger og synder
> og løftet dem høit op med sig paa et kors. Ikke min
> lykke og mit overmod, men min synd og min sorg, o
> søte Herre min —. Hun saa op, hvor krucifikset stod
> opløftet, svimlende høit over triumfbuen.
> — Mens morgensolen tændte de høie brogede ruter
> inderst i bunden av høikorets søileskog og glans som
> av røde og brune og grønne og blaa gimstener dim
> met lysglansen fra alteret og fra guldkisten bakom,
> hørte Kristin den sidste vigilie — ottesangen. Læsestyk
> kerne ved denne tjeneste visste hun var om Guds læge-
> domsjertegn ved hans kraft i hans trofaste ridder, kong
> Olav Haraldssøn. Hun løftet det fremmede syke barn
> op mot koret og bad for det.
> Men hun frøs saa hun hakket tænder efter det lange
> ophold i kirkekulden, og hun kjendte sig mat av fasten.
> Lugten av de mange mennesker og den kvalme tev av
> de syke og fattige blandet sig med vokslysosen og sank
> underlig fet og klam og tung nedover de knælende paa
> gulvet, kold i den kolde morgen. Men en tyk og blid
> og glad bondekone som hadde sittet og blundet litt ind
> til foten av pillaren like bak dem, med et bjørneskind
> under sætet og et andet over sine lamme ben, vaagnet
> nu og drog Kristins trætte hode bort paa sit rummelige
> fang: «Hvil litt nu, søster — du kan trænge det, tænker
> jeg —»
> Kristin sovnet i den fremmede kones skjød og drømte:
> Hun steg over dørstokken ind i den gamle aarestuen
> hjemme. Hun var ung og ugift, for hun saa sine egne
> tykke brune fletter som hang bare frem over skuldrene
> hendes. Hun var i følge med Erlend, for han rettet sig
> netop op efter han hadde gaat foran hende ind under
> dørkarmen.
> Ved aaren sat hendes far og skjeftet piler — hadde
> fanget fuldt med bundter av senetraad, og ved begge
> sider av ham laa der dynger av pilodder og tilspikkede
> skafter paa bænken. I det samme de traadte ind, bøiet
> han sig frem over glohaugen og skulde ta den lille tre
> bente malmkoppen som han altid brukte at smelte kvae
> i. Men braatt snappet han haanden til sig, ristet den
> i luften, og saa ståk han de brændte fingertupperne
> ind i munden og suget paa dem, mens han snudde ho
> det mot hende og Erlend og saa op paa dem med ryn
> lcede bryn og smil om læberne —.
> Saa vaagnet hun med ansigtet vaatt av graat.
> Hun knælte under høimessen, da erkebiskopen selv
> forrettet tjenesten foran hovedalteret. Virakskyerne
> bølget gjennem den tonende kirke, hvor nu broget
> solskin og vokslysglans blandet sit skin; den friske
> krydrede røkelsesduft sivet utover og døvet lugten av
> armod og sygdom. Med hjertet bristende fuldt av
> samkjendsel med flokken av arme og saare, som Gud hadde
> stillet hende ind i, bad hun i en rus av søsterlig ømhet
> for alle som var fattige med hende og led som hun
> selv hadde lidt —.


## English translation supplied to the annotator

> Simon came past the cookhouse door, stood for a moment and stared in. Sigurd said quickly that now he would get an awl and a buckle and mend properly what had broken on the saddle gear that morning.
> 
> “Do they have such things in the cookhouse here on the farm—” Simon flung at him, and went away again. Sigurd shook his head and nodded to the woman when Simon was out of sight.
> 
> Simon pushed the food dish away and remained sitting. He was so tired that he scarcely cared to rise. Yet at last he went over and threw himself onto the bed with boots and spurs on—but then thought that was a shame too: the bed was clean and good for being among common folk. He sat up and pulled off his footwear. Stiff and weary as he was, surely he ought to be able to sleep now; he was soaked through and freezing, but his face burned after the long ride against the storm.
> 
> He crept beneath the coverlet, turned and shifted the pillows—they smelled so strangely of fish. Then he lay half raised on one elbow.
> 
> His thoughts began going round in circles again. He had thought and thought during these days like an animal pacing around at the end of its tether.
> 
> Even if Erling Vidkunssøn had known that Gyrd and Gudmund Darre’s welfare too might be at stake if Erlend Nikulaussøn were broken into speaking—well, then it was surely no worse that Simon had tried every means to win the Bjarkøy knight’s help. Rather the opposite—a man surely owed it to his own brothers to stand together with them unto death, if need be. But he wished he knew whether Erling had known it. Simon weighed one thing against another. Erling could not possibly have been wholly ignorant that a rebellion was brewing. But what Erling knew—. Gyrd and Ulf, at any rate, did not seem to know whether that man was aware that they were involved. Yet Simon remembered that Erling had mentioned Haftor’s sons and advised him to seek help there, for it was rather their friends who had cause to be afraid—. Haftor’s sons were first cousins to Ulf Saksesøn and Helga. The nose is near the eyes—!
> 
> But even if Erling Vidkunssøn had believed that Simon was also thinking of his own brothers, that surely did not make what he had done any worse. And Erling might very well have understood that Simon knew nothing of his brothers’ danger. Besides, Simon himself had said—he remembered saying it to Stig—that he did not believe they could torture Erlend into speaking.
> 
> Even so, they might well have cause to fear Erlend’s mouth. Once he had kept silent through torture and captivity, he was just the man to betray himself afterward by a slip of the tongue. That would have been like him—.
> 
> Though Simon thought that was the one thing he could be sure Erlend would not do. He fell silent as stone whenever the conversation turned to this matter, precisely because he was afraid of being lured into a slip. Simon understood that Erlend had a violent, almost childish fear of breaking his faith—childish because Erlend plainly did not think it stained his honor in any significant way that he himself had revealed the whole undertaking to his whore. Such a thing, he surely thought, might happen to the best of men. So long as he himself held his tongue, he counted his shield unstained and his faith unbroken—and Simon had understood that Erlend was sensitive about his honor, insofar as he himself understood what honor and honorable conduct were. He had become almost senseless with despair and rage merely at the thought that any of his accomplices should be betrayed—now, so long afterward, and in such a manner that it could not possibly make any difference to the men whom he had sheltered with his life—and his honor and his property—through the child’s talk to the man who was those men’s closest kinsman—.
> 
> He would arrange it so that, if things went wrong, he would pay the penalty for them all—that Erlend had sworn upon the crucifix before all the men who had joined him in this undertaking. But that grown, sensible men could trust in such an oath—Erlend could hardly be blamed for that himself. Now that he knew everything about the conspiracy, Simon thought it the most insanely foolish thing he had ever heard of. Erlend had been willing to let himself be broken apart joint by joint in order to keep his oath according to its words. Meanwhile the secret lay in the hands of a ten-year-old boy—Erlend himself had seen to that. Nor was it likely to his credit that Sunniva Olavsdatter knew no more than she did—. Could any human being make sense of such a fellow—.
> 
> So if for one moment Simon had believed—yes, what Erlend and his wife thought he had believed—God knew that interpretation had lain very close at hand when Gaute came with this talk of having seen Simon’s seal beneath the treasonous letter. And those two might remember that Simon knew one thing and another about Erlend Nikulaussøn, so he had less cause than most men to think the best of that guest at all times. But they had probably long since forgotten how once he had come upon them and seen their shamelessness to the very bottom—.
> 
> So there was little sense in his lying here and feeling ashamed like a dog because he had wronged Erlend in his thoughts. God knew it was not because he wished to believe evil of his brother-in-law—he had only become miserable when the thought came to him. But he himself knew it was an insanely stupid suspicion—he would have realized at once, even without Kristin’s words, that it could not fit together like that. Almost as quickly as the suspicion—that Erlend had misused his seal—he had felt: no, Erlend could not possibly have done such a thing. Erlend had never in his life committed a dishonorable act that had forethought behind it—or made any sense—.
> 
> Simon twisted around in the bed and groaned. They had made him half foolish himself with all this folly. It tormented him so when he thought that Gaute had gone about for years believing this of him—it was unreasonable to take it so heavily. Even if he loved the boy, loved all Kristin’s sons—they were almost only children; need he take so much to heart what they judged of him!
> 
> And that he should become so boilingly angry when he thought of the men who had laid their hands upon the hilt of Erlend’s sword and sworn to follow their chieftain. If they had been such sheep that they could let themselves be dazzled by Erlend’s eloquence and bold bearing and believe that man had the makings of a chieftain, then it was only to be expected that they should behave like frightened sheep when the whole undertaking went awry. Yet he felt almost dizzy when he thought of what he had now learned at Dyfrin—so many men had dared place the peace of the realm and their own welfare in Erlend’s hands—and Haftor Olavssøn and Borgar Trondssøn—! And not one had had the courage to step forward and demand of the king that Erlend be granted honorable reconciliation and salvation for his hereditary estate. There were so many of them that, had they stood together, they could easily have forced it through. There was less both of sense and manly courage among the gentlemen-at-arms in Norway than he had believed—.
> 
> He was angry too because he himself had been kept entirely outside these counsels. Not that they could have brought him into such a headless undertaking. But that both Erlend and Gyrd had gone behind his back and concealed it—. He was surely as good a nobleman as any of the others and counted for no little in those districts where people knew him—.
> 
> In a way he granted that Gyrd was right. As Erlend had parted from his chieftainship, the man could not reasonably demand that his confederates step forward and name themselves in association with him. Simon knew that, had he found Gyrd alone, he would not have parted from his brothers as he had. But there lay that knight Ulf with his long legs stretched out and held forth about Erlend’s lack of sense—now afterward! And then Gudmund spoke up. Neither Gyrd nor Simon himself had ever before allowed the youngest brother to hold an opinion against them. But since he had married the priest’s bastard daughter, who had afterward been his own concubine, the lad had become so puffed up and proud and self-sufficient—Simon had sat staring himself nearly wild at Gudmund—he spoke so importantly, and his round red face looked like a child’s backside, so that Simon’s hands itched to thrash him—. In the end he had scarcely known himself what he said to the three men.
> 
> So now it was broken between him and his brothers. It felt as though he would bleed to death when he thought of it—as though bonds of flesh and blood had been torn apart. It impoverished him. Bare is the back of the brotherless—.
> 
> But however it was, in the midst of the violent quarrel he had suddenly understood—he did not know how—that Gyrd’s extinguished, numb manner was not merely because his brother was hard pressed for a little peace in his house. Gyrd still loved Helga, Simon had seen in a flash; that was what made his brother so strangely bound and powerless. And in an incomprehensible, mysterious way this awakened Simon’s fury against—yes, against all of life.
> 
> Simon buried his face in his hands. Yes, such good, obedient sons they had been. It had come easily both to Gyrd and to himself to conceive love for the brides whom their father came and said he had chosen for them. The old man had spoken to them very beautifully one evening, so that in the end they had both sat there quite abashed—about marriage and friendship and faithfulness between honorable, courtly spouses; yes, at last their father had mentioned prayers of intercession and masses. It was a pity their father had not known to counsel them also about forgetting—when friendship is broken and honor is dead and faithfulness a sin and a secret, shameful torment, and nothing remains after the bond but the bleeding wound that can never heal—.
> 
> After Erlend was set free, a kind of calm had fallen over Simon himself—only because a man cannot endure suffering as grievous as he had suffered during that time in Oslo. Either something happens—or else it grows better of itself.
> 
> He had not liked it when she moved into Jørundgaard with her husband and all their children, and he had to meet them and maintain friendship and kinship. But he comforted himself—it had been so much worse when he had had to live together with her in the way it is unbearable for a man to live with a woman he loves when she is neither his wife nor his blood kinswoman. And what had happened between Erlend and himself on the evening when they celebrated his brother-in-law’s release from the tower—he smoothed that over: Erlend had probably understood no more than half, and surely thought little about it. Erlend had such a rare gift for forgetting. And Simon himself had his farm and his wife, whom he loved, and his children.
> 
> He settled into peace in a fashion. It was not within his own power that he loved his wife’s sister. She had once been his betrothed—it was not he who had broken faith with her. At the time when he set his heart upon Kristin Lavransdatter, it had only been his duty to do so, since he believed that she was destined to become his wife. That he got the sister—that had been Ramborg’s counsel—and her father’s. Lavrans, wise man though he was, had not thought to ask whether Simon had forgotten. For that matter, Simon knew that not even from Lavrans would he have endured being asked. He had little gift for forgetting. That was not of his own making. And he had never said a word that he ought to have kept silent. He could not help it that the devil sought entry to him with sudden thoughts and dreams that offended the blood-bound bond—of his own will he had never surrendered himself to sinful thoughts of love. And he had acted as a faithful brother toward her and hers. He knew that himself.
> 
> At last he had managed to be fairly content with his fate.
> 
> So long as he knew that it was he who had served those two over there—Kristin and the man whom she chose before him. They had always been obliged to accept his support.
> 
> Now this had become otherwise. Kristin had risked life and the salvation of her soul to save his son’s life. It was as though every old wound had sprung open since he had allowed that to happen.
> 
> And afterward he had come to owe Erlend his life. And in return he had wronged him—against his will, only in his thoughts—but still!
> 
> “—et dimitte nobis debita nostra, sicut et nos dimittimus debitoribus nostris.” It was strange that the Lord had not also taught us to pray, “sicut et nos dimittimus creditoribus nostris.” He did not know whether that was proper Latin—he had never been strong in that language. But he knew that he had always been able to forgive his debtors at once. It seemed to him far harder to forgive the man who had fastened a debt upon his neck—.
> 
> And now, when they could call themselves quits—he and those two as well—he felt every old grievance that he had trampled underfoot for years spring up and come alive—.
> 
> He could no longer push Erlend aside in his thoughts—a foolish boor who could see or learn or remember or think nothing. Now the other man oppressed his mind precisely because no one could know what Erlend saw and thought and remembered—he was wholly incalculable.
> 
> Many a man receives what is destined for another, but no man receives another’s fate—.
> 
> That was truly said.
> 
> He had cared for his young betrothed. Had he won her, he would surely have become a contented man; they would probably have come to live well together. And she would have remained as she was when they first met: gentle and modest, sensible, so that a man might willingly consult her even in weightier matters, a little willful in small things but otherwise compliant, accustomed as she was from her father’s hands to let herself be guided and supported and protected. But then this man gained power over her—one who was unfit to govern himself and had never protected anything. He had ravaged her sweet maidenhood, broken her proud composure, burst open this woman’s spirit and forced her to stretch and strain every faculty she possessed to its utmost. She had been obliged to rise in defense of her lover as a small bird defends its nest with trembling body and shrill voice when anyone approaches its brood. Her lovely, slender body had seemed to Simon created to be borne up and tenderly sheltered by a man’s arms—he had seen it taut with wild will, while her heart hammered within her from courage and fear and battle-fury and she fought for husband and children, as even the dove can become wild and fearless when she has young.
> 
> Had he himself been her husband, had she lived for fifteen winters with his honorable goodwill, he knew well that she would have risen for him too if he had fallen into misfortune. With prudence and courage she would have stood at his side. But never would he have seen the stone face she turned toward him that evening in Oslo, when she sat telling him that she had been away to inspect that house. His own name he would surely never have heard her cry out in such wild distress and lamentation. And it was not the honorable and righteous love of his youth that had answered in his heart. That wildness which rose and screamed against her wildness—he would never have known that anything like it could dwell within his own mind if matters between him and her had gone as their fathers had arranged—.
> 
> Her countenance when she passed him and went out into the night to find help for his child—she would not have dared go then had she not been Erlend’s wife, who had been obliged to accustom herself to act without fear even when her heart shook with terror. Her tear-wet smile when she woke him and said that the boy was calling for his father—only one who knows both what it is to lose a battle and to win can smile with such breaking sweetness—.
> 
> It was Erlend’s wife whom he loved—as he loved her now. But then his love must be sinful, and so it must surely be as it was—that he was unhappy. For he was so unhappy that at times he felt only a great astonishment—that it was he himself who had it thus and who saw no way out of his misery.
> 
> When he trampled upon his own honor and noble decorum and reminded Erling Vidkunssøn of things no honorable man would have hinted that he knew—he had not done that for brothers or kinsmen, but only for her. Only for her sake had he endured begging the other man as the lepers beg at the church door in the market towns and display their hideous wounds.
> 
> He had thought—one day she should learn of it. Not everything, not how deeply he had humbled himself. But when they had both grown old, he had thought he would say it to Kristin: I helped you as well as I could, because I remembered how dearly I wished you well in the days when I was your betrothed—.
> 
> There was one thing he did not dare turn his thoughts toward. Had Erlend said anything to Kristin? Yes, he had thought that one day she should learn it from his own mouth—I have never forgotten that I loved you when we were young. But if she knew it and it was from her husband that she had heard of it—no, then he thought he could endure no more—.

> 
> Simon cried out, wildly and chokingly, and suddenly flung himself onto the other side of the bed.
> 
> God help him! Now it was he himself who lay here, stripped naked, violated, bleeding from torment and trembling with shame—.
>

> 
> He would allow them a good lead. Otherwise he too would soon have to begin thinking of the journey home—.
> 
> The next time Simon came fully to himself, he was being carried onward through the winter night. Pain throbbed through his arm, and fever rose and fell within him. Yet beneath the fever he saw Kristin as she had been when she was young.

>

If it was a sin for him to say it to his wife’s sister before he died, then the Mother of God must obtain forgiveness for him for this too. >

>

> 
> They reached the pilgrims’ hospice toward morning, and the two monks who managed it took charge of the sick man. But when he had revived somewhat under their care and the fever delirium had lessened, Simon Andressøn stubbornly insisted on borrowing a sleigh and continuing north.
> 
> The going was good, and they changed horses along the way, traveled all night, and reached Formo at daybreak the next morning. Simon had lain dozing beneath everything they had spread over him. It pressed upon him so—sometimes it felt as though he must be crushed beneath great slabs of rock—and his head hurt so badly. At times he seemed to disappear. Then the pains began raging in him again—it was as though his body swelled and swelled, became inconceivably vast and was about to burst to pieces. His arm throbbed and throbbed—.
> 
> He tried to walk by himself from the sleigh and indoors—with his sound arm across Jon’s shoulder, while Sigurd walked behind to support him. Simon perceived that the men’s faces were gray and grimy with weariness—they had spent two nights in succession in the saddle. He wanted to say something to them about it, but his tongue would not obey him. He stumbled over the threshold and pitched into the room—with a roar of pain when his monstrously swollen arm struck against something. Sweat streamed from him as he swallowed the groans that sought to escape while he was undressed and helped into bed.
> 
> Not long afterward he saw Kristin Lavransdatter standing by the stove, pounding something with a club in a wooden bowl. The sound went through his head. She poured from a small pot into a cup and dripped drops into it from a glass bottle she took from her chest—emptied what she had crushed in the bowl into the pot and set it by the fire. So quiet and deft was her manner—.
> 
> Now she came over to the bed with the cup in her hand. She walked so lightly. She was as erect and beautiful as the maiden had been, the slender housewife with the lean, grave countenance beneath the linen wimple. He was swollen at the back of his neck too; it hurt when she slid one arm underneath and raised him a little. She supported his head against her breast while holding the cup to his mouth with her left hand.
> 
> Simon smiled a little, and when she carefully let his head sink back onto the pillows, he caught one of her hands with his sound hand. It was no longer either soft or white, that fine, long, narrow woman’s hand.
> 
> “You can scarcely sew silk with those fingers of yours now,” said Simon. “But good and gentle they are—and deliciously cool is your hand, Kristin!” He laid it upon his forehead. Kristin remained standing thus until she felt the palm grow warm; then she changed hands and gently pressed the other against his burning-hot forehead, all the way up beneath the roots of his hair.
> 
> “Your arm is ugly, Simon,” she said. “But with God’s help it may yet go well.”
> 
> “I fear, Kristin, that even so skillful a healer as you cannot cure me,” said Simon. But his expression was almost cheerful. The drink was beginning to work; he felt the pains far less. But his eyes felt so strange—as though he could not control them—he thought he must be lying there squinting with one eye in each direction—.
> 
> “It will go with me as it has been ordained,” he said as before.
> 
> Kristin returned to her pots—spread a paste upon strips of linen, came back and wrapped the hot bandages around his arm from his fingertips, all the way behind his back and across his breast, where the swelling ran in red streaks from the armpit. It hurt at first, but soon gave relief. She wrapped woolen cloths over it and placed soft down pillows beneath the arm. Simon asked what she had put on the bandages.
> 
> “Oh, various things—comfrey root and celandine,” said Kristin. “If only it had been summer, so I could have taken them fresh from my herb garden. But I had plenty—thanks be to God—I have not needed any of it earlier this winter.”
> 
> “What was it you once told me about celandine—you had heard it from the abbess when you were in the convent—about the name—”
> 
> “Do you mean that in every tongue it bears a name signifying swallow-flower—from the Greek sea all the way here to the northern lands?”
> 
> “Yes—for it comes into flower in every land when the swallows wake from their winter sleep?” Simon pressed his lips a little more firmly together. By then he would long since have lain in the earth—.
> 
> “I wish to have my resting place by the church here,” he said, “if it should go so that I die now, Kristin. I should gladly have lived long enough to see two sons on my farm—”
> 
> Kristin said she had sent word south to Dyfrin that he had become so ill. Gaute—he had already ridden that morning.
> 
> “You did not send that child off alone?” Simon asked in alarm.
> 
> She had had no one at hand whom she believed capable of keeping pace with Gaute on Rauden, she answered. Simon said it would likely be a hard journey for Ramborg—if only she did not hurry more than she could bear. “But I should dearly like to see my children—”
> 
> A while later he began speaking of his children again. He mentioned Arngjerd—whether he had done wrong in refusing the offer from the people at Eiken. But the man had seemed too old to him—and he had feared that Grunde was said to be somewhat violent when drunk. Arngjerd—he had wished to place her in the safest circumstances. Now it would be Gyrd and Gudmund who would decide her marriage. “Tell my brothers, Kristin—I sent them greeting, and they must look carefully to this matter. If you would take her to you at Jørundgaard for a time, I shall thank you where I lie. And if Ramborg marries again before Arngjerd is provided for, then you must take her to you, Kristin. You must not think that Ramborg has ever been anything but kind to her—but if she is to have both a stepmother and a stepfather, I fear she might be regarded more as a servant woman than as—you remember, I was married to Halfrid when I became her father—”
> 
> Kristin gently laid her hand upon Simon’s and promised that she would do everything she could for the maiden. She remembered all she had seen—difficult was the lot of those children whose father was a great lord and who had been begotten in adultery. Orm and Margret, Ulf Haldorssøn—. She stroked and stroked Simon’s hand.
> 
> “It is not certain that you will die this time either, brother-in-law,” she said, smiling a little. Even now an afterglow of the maiden’s sweet and gentle smile could pass over the lean, stern face of the wife. You sweet young Kristin—.
> 
> Simon did not have such a high fever that evening, and he felt less pain, he said. When Kristin changed the bandage upon his arm, it was not so swollen; the flesh was softer, and the marks of her fingers remained for a while when she carefully pressed it.
> 
> Kristin sent the servants to bed. Jon Daalk, who insisted on keeping watch over his master, she allowed to lie down on the bench. She had the chest-bench with the carved back moved forward before the bed and set there, leaning into the corner. Simon dozed and slept—once, when he woke, he saw that she had found herself a spindle. She sat so erect, having tucked the distaff with its head of wool beneath her left arm; her fingers twisted the thread, the spindle sank and sank beside her long, slender lap—then she wound up the thread, twisted again, the spindle sank—he fell asleep watching it—.
> 
> When he woke again toward morning, she was sitting in the same way and spinning. The light from the candle, which she had placed so that the bed curtains shaded him, fell directly upon her face. It was so pale and still; the full, soft mouth had become narrow and firmly closed, and she sat with lowered eyes, spinning. She could not see that he lay awake staring at her from the shadow of the bed curtains. She looked so utterly sorrowful that Simon felt as though his heart bled within him while he lay looking at her.
> 
> She rose, went away and tended the fire. So soundlessly. When she returned, she peered behind the bed curtain—and met his open eyes in the darkness.
> 
> “How is it with you now, Simon?” she asked softly.
> 
> “Well—it is well with me now.”
> 
> But he thought he felt tenderness beneath his left arm too now—and beneath his chin when he moved his head. No, it was probably only something he imagined—.
> 
> She would surely never think she had lost anything when she rejected his love—on that account he might safely tell her of it. It could not possibly make her more melancholy. He would tell her before he died—just once: I have loved you all these years—.
> 
> The fever rose again. And his left arm hurt—.
> 
> “You must try to sleep again, Simon. Perhaps you will quickly grow better now,” she said quietly.

> “I have slept a great deal today—.” He began speaking of his children again—the three whom he had and loved so dearly—and the unborn one. > In the morning, when she loosened the bandage, Simon looked calmly into her despairing face. “No, Kristin, there was too much corruption and impurity in the arm already—and I had been chilled before I came into your hands. > “You should not have traveled all that long way,” she said faintly.
> 
> “No man lives longer than he is destined to,” Simon answered as before. “I wished to come home—. There is one thing and another to discuss—how matters are to be arranged after me.”
> 
> He laughed a little.
> 
> “All fires burn out at last.”
> 
> Kristin looked at him with tear-bright eyes. He had always had so many proverbs upon his tongue. She looked down into his red-blotched face. The heavy cheeks and the folds beneath his chin seemed to have sunk and lay in deep creases. > 
> When full daylight entered the room, Kristin saw that Simon had grown thin around the nose—a stripe of white ran downward toward each corner of his mouth.
> 
> She went to the little glass pane and stood there swallowing her tears. It glittered and shone golden-green in the thick rime upon the window. Outside it must be as beautiful a winter day today as it had been all week—.
> 
> That was the sign of death, she knew—.
> 
> She returned and slipped her hand beneath the coverlet—he was swollen around the ankles and upward along the calves.
> 
> “Would you—would you have us send for Sira Eirik now?” she asked softly.
> 
> “Yes, this evening,” Simon answered.
> 
> He had to speak of it before he confessed and received the sacrament. Afterward he must try to turn his thoughts another way.
> 
> “Strange it is that it will surely be you who must tend my corpse,” said Simon. “And I fear I shall not make a very beautiful corpse.”
> 
> Kristin bit back a sob. She went away and prepared another cooling drink. But Simon said:
> 
> “I do not like these drinks of yours, Kristin—my thoughts grow so unclear after them.”
> 
> After a while he asked her to give him a little after all.
> 
> “But do not put too much of the numbing thing into it. I must speak with you about a matter.”
> 
> He drank and lay waiting for the pain to ease enough that he could speak clearly and calmly with her.
> 
> “Will you not have us bring Sira Eirik to you—so that he can speak to you words that might now give you comfort?”
> 
> “Yes, soon. But there is something I should have told you first.”
> 
> He lay silent for a little. Then he said:
> 
> “Tell Erlend Nikulaussøn that I have regretted every day the words I spoke to him the last time we parted. Childishly and meanly did I behave toward my brother-in-law that night—. Greet him and say—ask him to forgive me for this.”
> 
> Kristin sat with bowed head—Simon saw her become blood-red beneath the linen.
> 
> “You will carry this message to your husband?” he asked.
> 
> She nodded slightly. Then Simon said again:
> 
> “If Erlend does not come to my funeral feast, then you must seek him out, Kristin, and say this to him.”
> 
> Kristin sat silent and dark red in the face.
> 
> “You will not refuse to do what I ask of you now that I am to die?” Simon Andressøn asked.
> 
> “No,” the woman whispered. “I shall—do it—”
> 
> “It is ill for your sons, Kristin, that there is enmity between their father and mother,” Simon began again. “I wonder whether you have seen how greatly this troubles them. It is hard for those fine lads to know that their parents are upon every tongue in the district.”
> 
> Kristin answered harshly and softly:
> 
> “Erlend left our sons—not I. First my sons lost their footing in the districts where they were born to lineage and hereditary land. If they must now endure gossip about their home here in the valley from which I come—it is not I who caused it.”

> 
> “I do not forget that, Kristin—you have a right to complain of many things—ill has Erlend provided for his own. A traitor to the crown is what the man is called who fails in such a cause—but if he succeeds, people speak very differently. Half of Norway thought as Erlend did then—that we were ill served by sharing a king with the Swedes and that the son of Knut Porse likely had other metal in him than this pampered youth—if only we could have brought young lord Haakon to us in his infancy. Many stood behind Erlend then and pulled the rope with him—they let go and crept into hiding when the matter came to light—so did my own brothers and many others who are now called good knights and men-at-arms. Erlend alone had to fall—. And then, Kristin, your husband showed that he was a noble and courageous man—however he may have behaved both this way and that before and since—”
> 
> Kristin sat still and trembling.
> 
> “I think, Kristin, if it is on account of this matter that you have spoken bitter words to your husband, then you must swallow them again. > 
> Kristin answered softly:
> 
> “I had cast my lot in with his then; what would have become of me afterward if I had separated my cause from Erlend’s?”
> 
> “Look at me, Kristin,” said Simon Darre, “and answer me truthfully. Had I held your father to his given word—chosen to take you as you were—if I had told you that I would never remind you of your shame, but that I would not release you—what would you have done then?”

> “I do not know.”
> 
> Simon laughed harshly.
>

> 
> “I tell you, for your sons’ sake you must seek reconciliation with your husband. If you bear the lesser share of wrong, then surely it is easier for you to offer Erlend your hand,” he said more gently.
> 
> “It is easier for you than for Erlend Nikulaussøn, where he sits up at Haugen in poverty,” Simon said again.
> 
> “It is not easy for me,” she whispered. “I think I have shown that I can do something for my children. I have struggled and struggled for them—”
>

> 
> Kristin nodded.
>

> 
> Kristin shook her head.
>

> “I shall do as you wish, Simon,” Kristin said softly.

> Kristin kept watch over the dying man that night as well. Toward morning she fell asleep for a moment. She woke at a strange sound—Simon lay moaning softly and quietly. It seized her violently when she heard it—that he lamented, quietly and painfully like a wretched, abandoned child, now that he surely thought no one could hear him. She bent down and kissed him many times upon the face. She could smell that his breath and his body already had a sickly, dead odor. But when daylight came, she saw that his eyes were still alive and clear and steadfast.

> He suffered terrible pain, she understood, when Jon and Sigurd lifted him in a sheet while she tended his bed and made it as soft and comfortable as she could. He had wanted no food for a day and a night now, but he was very thirsty.
> 
> When she had settled him, he asked her to make the sign of the cross over him: “Now I cannot move my left arm either.”

> When we sign ourselves with the mark of the cross, or anything that we wish to shelter with the cross, then we must remember why the cross was hallowed and what it signifies, and remember that through the Lord’s torments and death this sign received honor and power—.
> 
> Simon remembered that he had once heard someone read this aloud. He had probably not been accustomed to think much when he crossed his breast or marked the cross upon his houses and his property. He felt unready and ill prepared to depart from this world—he must comfort himself that he had made himself ready as well as there was time for, through confession, and he had received the last sacrament. Ramborg—but she was so young; perhaps she would be far happier with another man. His children—God must take them into his protection—and Gyrd would manage their welfare faithfully and wisely.
> 
> And then he must entrust himself to God, who judges a man not according to his worth but by His grace—.
> 
> Later that day Sigrid Andresdatter and Geirmund of Kruke came. Then for an instant a loud sob tore from her—afterward she bent down and again kissed the wretched body that had already begun to decay.
> 
> Simon lay quietly. The fever and the pains were now much less severe. He lay thinking that it could surely not be long before he was delivered.
> 
> He himself wondered that he had spoken to Kristin as he had. That was not what he had meant to say to her. But he had been unable to speak otherwise. At moments he was almost vexed by it.
> 
> But now the mortification must soon reach his heart. A man’s heart is the first thing that quickens in the womb and the last thing in him to fall still. But now it must surely soon grow still within him—.
> 
> In the evening he lay delirious. A few times he cried aloud in anguish, so that it was terrible to hear. At other times he lay laughing softly and spoke his own name, Kristin thought—but Sigrid, who sat bent over him, whispered to her that he was surely speaking of a boy, their cousin, who had been such a good friend to him in childhood. Around midnight he grew calm and seemed to sleep. Then Sigrid persuaded Kristin to lie down for a while on the other bed in the room.
> 
> She woke at unrest in the chamber—it was shortly before dawn—and then she heard that the death struggle had begun. Simon had lost his speech, but he still knew her; she could see it in his eyes. Then it was as though steel broke within them—they rolled upward beneath the lids. Yet for a while he still lay rattling and alive. The priest had come and read the prayers for the dying; the two women sat beside the bed, and the whole household was in the room. A little before midday he breathed his last.
